[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.0, F41.1, F41.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I came in because things have been feeling kind of off, and what finally made me actually call EAP was this thing that keeps happening at work. Every little sudden noise in the office makes me jump way harder than it should. Like, if someone shuts a file drawer too loudly or drops a coffee mug in the break area, I don’t just flinch a little — I jump like something really serious happened.\n\nAnd then my chest starts racing, and it keeps going for way longer than makes sense. I know, logically, it’s just a drawer or a mug, but my body reacts like it’s a big deal before I can even think about it. It’s embarrassing too, because I’m a supervisor, so I’m supposed to be composed, you know? But lately these small bangs just set me off, and I don’t feel like I settle back down quickly. That’s why I thought I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Do these jumpiness and racing heart episodes only happen in response to sudden noises, or do they occur at other times as well?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the sudden noises, honestly. That’s the pattern I’ve noticed the clearest. Like, it isn’t that I’m just sitting there and my heart randomly starts pounding out of nowhere—at least not that I’ve really tracked. What made me call EAP was that every little bang in the office, like a file drawer shutting too hard or someone dropping a coffee mug, makes me jump way more than it should.\n\nAnd it’s not just a quick startle and then I’m fine. My chest starts racing, and it keeps going longer than makes sense for something so small. I’ll be trying to act normal, like I’m still focused on whatever work conversation is happening, but physically I’m still kind of keyed up. So… yeah, I’d say it’s mainly in response to sudden noises, at least that’s what I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or on edge in situations that are not related to sudden noises?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, it’s not as dramatic as the sudden noise thing, but I’ve noticed I’m kind of on alert in the office in general now.\n\nLike, when I’m supposed to be focused on my team or just getting through the day, part of my attention is somewhere else. I keep checking where my boss is, which is not something I used to do so much. I’ll catch myself noticing if he’s in his office, or walking around, or if I hear footsteps nearby. And I also keep clocking where the exits are, which sounds weird saying it out loud because I know I’m just at work, but it’s like I want to know how I’d get out if I needed to.\n\nAnd another thing is I really don’t like when people come up behind me now. If I’m at my desk or talking with someone, I’ll sort of position myself so I can see the hallway or see who’s coming. It’s not like I’m thinking through it in a calm, logical way every time — it’s more like I realize I’ve been scanning the room or checking behind me instead of actually listening as well as I should be. So yeah, I guess I do feel on edge even when there isn’t a noise. It’s just this constant checking.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel this way, on edge and constantly checking your surroundings, when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way, no. I mean, I can be a little wound up in general, but the constant checking thing is mainly when I’m in the office.\n\nThat’s where I notice it the most — I’m supposed to be focused on my team or whatever I’m working on, and instead I’m tracking where my boss is, whether he’s in his office or moving around, where the exits are, and if anyone is coming up behind me. I’ll catch myself positioning my chair or standing in a way where I can see the hallway, and it’s like I’m not even fully choosing to do it.\n\nOutside of work, I don’t think I’m scanning like that as much. It’s the office setting that seems to bring it out.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode where you felt an intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes, with symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, or feeling like you were going to pass out?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re describing, where it just suddenly comes on and peaks within a few minutes like a full episode.\n\nI mean, my heart does race when I get startled by a loud noise, like I was saying, and I feel really uncomfortable and keyed up after that. But if you mean something where I’m just sitting there and then suddenly I’m overwhelmed with fear, sweating, feeling like I might pass out or something like that… I can’t really say that’s happened.\n\nAt least, I haven’t noticed it as a separate kind of episode. It’s more tied to being startled or being on edge at work, not like random waves that peak out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or on edge at home or in social situations outside of work?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… or at least not in that same specific way. Like, at home I’m not constantly checking doors or watching where people are. And if I’m out with friends or just in a normal social situation, I don’t think I’m scanning the room like that.\n\nThe thing I keep coming back to is the office. That’s where it feels like I can’t fully settle. I’ll be trying to pay attention to my team or whatever’s going on, and then I realize I’m also tracking where my boss is — like whether he’s in his office or moving around — and I’m aware of where the exits are. I also really notice if someone is coming up behind me, and I’ll kind of position myself so that doesn’t happen, or so I can see it coming.\n\nSo outside of work, I might feel a little tense sometimes, but the constant checking, the “where is everyone, how do I get out, who’s behind me” thing… that’s mostly at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about work-related issues when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate that out. I mean, I’ll think about work when I’m not there, but I don’t know if I’d call it worrying in the sense of sitting at home spiraling about emails or sales numbers or normal work stuff.\n\nIt’s more like… if I know I have to go in the next day, I might find myself thinking ahead to what the office is going to feel like, or whether things are going to be awkward, or whether I’m going to be able to just get through the day without feeling so tense. But I wouldn’t say it’s constant all evening or anything like that.\n\nSo, yeah, work crosses my mind outside of work, but I haven’t really noticed it being this big separate thing where I’m obsessing over work problems at home. It’s more tied to having to go back there, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety at work more about not doing your job well or is it more about being in the office itself?\n\nPatient: It’s more about being in the office itself, I think. Not that I don’t care about doing my job well — I do, and I still try to focus on my team and what we’re supposed to be doing — but the anxiety doesn’t feel like, “Oh no, I’m going to mess up this report,” or “I’m not performing well enough.”\n\nIt’s more like once I’m physically there, in the office, I can’t fully relax into what I’m doing. I’ll be talking to someone or trying to listen in a meeting, and part of my attention is still somewhere else, like checking where my boss is, whether he’s in his office or walking around, where the exits are, and whether someone is coming up behind me. I know that probably sounds excessive, but it just feels automatic now.\n\nSo, yeah, I’d say it’s less about my actual job performance and more about being in that space and feeling like I have to keep track of everything around me.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific in the office that you think might be causing you to feel this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if it’s one object or one room exactly, but it’s definitely tied to being there, in the office, and especially to where my boss is. Like if I’m at my desk or talking to someone on my team, part of me is still checking whether he’s in his office, whether he’s walking around, whether he’s nearby. It’s like I’m trying to keep a mental map of him without meaning to.\n\nAnd then it’s the layout too, I guess — where the exits are, who’s behind me, whether someone could come up behind me without me noticing. I’ll choose where I stand or sit based on that sometimes, like so I can see the hallway or not have my back totally exposed. Even when I’m supposed to just be focused on my team or a conversation, some part of my attention is off tracking all of that.\n\nSo I don’t know if I’d say there’s one specific thing causing it, but being in that office now makes me feel like I have to keep checking everything.\n\nDoctor: Is there a particular person at work, such as your boss, who triggers your anxiety or makes you feel more on edge?\n\nPatient: Yeah… my boss, mostly. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m blaming him for every single thing I feel at work, because it’s also the office itself now, like the whole setup feels different to me. But if you’re asking whether there’s a specific person who makes me more on edge, then yes, it’s him.\n\nWhen I’m in the office now, I keep checking where he is without really deciding to do it. Like I’ll be trying to talk to someone on my team or focus on whatever we’re working on, and part of my brain is still tracking, “Is he in his office? Is he walking down the hall? Is he behind me?” It’s not like I’m sitting there staring at him, but I’m aware of him in this constant way.\n\nAnd then along with that, I’m also checking the exits and whether anyone is coming up behind me. I’ll notice where I’m standing, where the door is, whether my back is turned. It feels kind of embarrassing to say because I know I’m supposed to just be doing my job, but I feel like I’m scanning the room all the time. So, yeah, my boss is definitely the person who seems to set that off the most.\n\nDoctor: Has something specific happened at work that you think might have triggered you to start feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah. It started after this late-night confrontation with my regional boss. That’s really the point where I noticed everything changed.\n\nIt’s been about two weeks now, maybe just over or right around that, but basically since that night. Before that, work was stressful in the normal way, you know, deadlines and people needing things, but I wasn’t walking into the office already bracing myself. After that confrontation, it was like my body started treating the office differently.\n\nI keep thinking, “Okay, it’s been two weeks, I should be over it,” but that’s the marker in my mind. That late-night thing with him happened, and since then I’ve felt this shift when I’m there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel less anxious at work if your boss were no longer there?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I probably would, at least somewhat. I don’t know if it would make the office feel completely normal again, because now I’m kind of keyed into the whole place — like the hallways, the exits, who’s behind me, that kind of thing. But he’s definitely the main thing I keep checking for.\n\nLike when I’m there, even if I’m supposed to be focused on my team or listening to someone talk, part of me is still going, “Where is he? Is he in his office? Is he coming out? Is he nearby?” And then I’m also looking at where the exits are or whether someone can come up behind me. It’s not like I’m trying to be dramatic about it, it’s just happening in the background all the time.\n\nSo if he weren’t there, I think that constant checking would probably drop down a lot. I might still feel tense in the office for a while, but I wouldn’t be tracking him every few seconds, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious if you were to encounter your boss in a setting outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think I probably would. I mean, I don’t know exactly because I haven’t really run into him outside of work like that, but I imagine my body would still react before I even had time to think it through.\n\nAt work, what happens is I keep checking where he is, even when I’m supposed to be focused on my team or having a normal conversation. Like part of me is still tracking him — where his office is, whether he’s coming down the hall, whether he’s behind me. And then it’s not just him, it’s also where the exits are, where I’m standing, if someone can come up behind me. It’s like I’m not fully in the room anymore because I’m doing all that in the background.\n\nSo if I saw him somewhere else, like a restaurant or a store or something, I think I’d probably start doing the same kind of thing — noticing where he is, where I am, how to get out, whether he’s coming closer. I don’t think I’d want to have that reaction, and I’d probably try to act normal, but yeah, I think I’d be anxious.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I don’t think there was actual physical harm, no. Like, he didn’t hit me or grab me or anything like that.

As far as physical threats… I’m not totally sure how to put it. I don’t remember him saying something clearly like, “I’m going to hurt you,” if that’s what you mean. It felt physically intimidating in the moment, but I don’t want to make it sound like there was a specific threat of violence if there wasn’t one.

So I guess the most accurate answer is: no actual physical harm, and I’m not sure there was a direct physical threat. It was more the way the situation felt — late at night, tense, hard to leave — but I can’t point to a clear physical threat.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.0, F41.1, F41.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I came in because things have been feeling kind of off, and what finally made me actually call EAP was this thing that keeps happening at work. Every little sudden noise in the office makes me jump way harder than it should. Like, if someone shuts a file drawer too loudly or drops a coffee mug in the break area, I don’t just flinch a little — I jump like something really serious happened.\n\nAnd then my chest starts racing, and it keeps going for way longer than makes sense. I know, logically, it’s just a drawer or a mug, but my body reacts like it’s a big deal before I can even think about it. It’s embarrassing too, because I’m a supervisor, so I’m supposed to be composed, you know? But lately these small bangs just set me off, and I don’t feel like I settle back down quickly. That’s why I thought I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Do these jumpiness and racing heart episodes only happen in response to sudden noises, or do they occur at other times as well?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the sudden noises, honestly. That’s the pattern I’ve noticed the clearest. Like, it isn’t that I’m just sitting there and my heart randomly starts pounding out of nowhere—at least not that I’ve really tracked. What made me call EAP was that every little bang in the office, like a file drawer shutting too hard or someone dropping a coffee mug, makes me jump way more than it should.\n\nAnd it’s not just a quick startle and then I’m fine. My chest starts racing, and it keeps going longer than makes sense for something so small. I’ll be trying to act normal, like I’m still focused on whatever work conversation is happening, but physically I’m still kind of keyed up. So… yeah, I’d say it’s mainly in response to sudden noises, at least that’s what I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or on edge in situations that are not related to sudden noises?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, it’s not as dramatic as the sudden noise thing, but I’ve noticed I’m kind of on alert in the office in general now.\n\nLike, when I’m supposed to be focused on my team or just getting through the day, part of my attention is somewhere else. I keep checking where my boss is, which is not something I used to do so much. I’ll catch myself noticing if he’s in his office, or walking around, or if I hear footsteps nearby. And I also keep clocking where the exits are, which sounds weird saying it out loud because I know I’m just at work, but it’s like I want to know how I’d get out if I needed to.\n\nAnd another thing is I really don’t like when people come up behind me now. If I’m at my desk or talking with someone, I’ll sort of position myself so I can see the hallway or see who’s coming. It’s not like I’m thinking through it in a calm, logical way every time — it’s more like I realize I’ve been scanning the room or checking behind me instead of actually listening as well as I should be. So yeah, I guess I do feel on edge even when there isn’t a noise. It’s just this constant checking.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel this way, on edge and constantly checking your surroundings, when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way, no. I mean, I can be a little wound up in general, but the constant checking thing is mainly when I’m in the office.\n\nThat’s where I notice it the most — I’m supposed to be focused on my team or whatever I’m working on, and instead I’m tracking where my boss is, whether he’s in his office or moving around, where the exits are, and if anyone is coming up behind me. I’ll catch myself positioning my chair or standing in a way where I can see the hallway, and it’s like I’m not even fully choosing to do it.\n\nOutside of work, I don’t think I’m scanning like that as much. It’s the office setting that seems to bring it out.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode where you felt an intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes, with symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, or feeling like you were going to pass out?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re describing, where it just suddenly comes on and peaks within a few minutes like a full episode.\n\nI mean, my heart does race when I get startled by a loud noise, like I was saying, and I feel really uncomfortable and keyed up after that. But if you mean something where I’m just sitting there and then suddenly I’m overwhelmed with fear, sweating, feeling like I might pass out or something like that… I can’t really say that’s happened.\n\nAt least, I haven’t noticed it as a separate kind of episode. It’s more tied to being startled or being on edge at work, not like random waves that peak out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or on edge at home or in social situations outside of work?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… or at least not in that same specific way. Like, at home I’m not constantly checking doors or watching where people are. And if I’m out with friends or just in a normal social situation, I don’t think I’m scanning the room like that.\n\nThe thing I keep coming back to is the office. That’s where it feels like I can’t fully settle. I’ll be trying to pay attention to my team or whatever’s going on, and then I realize I’m also tracking where my boss is — like whether he’s in his office or moving around — and I’m aware of where the exits are. I also really notice if someone is coming up behind me, and I’ll kind of position myself so that doesn’t happen, or so I can see it coming.\n\nSo outside of work, I might feel a little tense sometimes, but the constant checking, the “where is everyone, how do I get out, who’s behind me” thing… that’s mostly at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about work-related issues when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate that out. I mean, I’ll think about work when I’m not there, but I don’t know if I’d call it worrying in the sense of sitting at home spiraling about emails or sales numbers or normal work stuff.\n\nIt’s more like… if I know I have to go in the next day, I might find myself thinking ahead to what the office is going to feel like, or whether things are going to be awkward, or whether I’m going to be able to just get through the day without feeling so tense. But I wouldn’t say it’s constant all evening or anything like that.\n\nSo, yeah, work crosses my mind outside of work, but I haven’t really noticed it being this big separate thing where I’m obsessing over work problems at home. It’s more tied to having to go back there, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety at work more about not doing your job well or is it more about being in the office itself?\n\nPatient: It’s more about being in the office itself, I think. Not that I don’t care about doing my job well — I do, and I still try to focus on my team and what we’re supposed to be doing — but the anxiety doesn’t feel like, “Oh no, I’m going to mess up this report,” or “I’m not performing well enough.”\n\nIt’s more like once I’m physically there, in the office, I can’t fully relax into what I’m doing. I’ll be talking to someone or trying to listen in a meeting, and part of my attention is still somewhere else, like checking where my boss is, whether he’s in his office or walking around, where the exits are, and whether someone is coming up behind me. I know that probably sounds excessive, but it just feels automatic now.\n\nSo, yeah, I’d say it’s less about my actual job performance and more about being in that space and feeling like I have to keep track of everything around me.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific in the office that you think might be causing you to feel this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if it’s one object or one room exactly, but it’s definitely tied to being there, in the office, and especially to where my boss is. Like if I’m at my desk or talking to someone on my team, part of me is still checking whether he’s in his office, whether he’s walking around, whether he’s nearby. It’s like I’m trying to keep a mental map of him without meaning to.\n\nAnd then it’s the layout too, I guess — where the exits are, who’s behind me, whether someone could come up behind me without me noticing. I’ll choose where I stand or sit based on that sometimes, like so I can see the hallway or not have my back totally exposed. Even when I’m supposed to just be focused on my team or a conversation, some part of my attention is off tracking all of that.\n\nSo I don’t know if I’d say there’s one specific thing causing it, but being in that office now makes me feel like I have to keep checking everything.\n\nDoctor: Is there a particular person at work, such as your boss, who triggers your anxiety or makes you feel more on edge?\n\nPatient: Yeah… my boss, mostly. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m blaming him for every single thing I feel at work, because it’s also the office itself now, like the whole setup feels different to me. But if you’re asking whether there’s a specific person who makes me more on edge, then yes, it’s him.\n\nWhen I’m in the office now, I keep checking where he is without really deciding to do it. Like I’ll be trying to talk to someone on my team or focus on whatever we’re working on, and part of my brain is still tracking, “Is he in his office? Is he walking down the hall? Is he behind me?” It’s not like I’m sitting there staring at him, but I’m aware of him in this constant way.\n\nAnd then along with that, I’m also checking the exits and whether anyone is coming up behind me. I’ll notice where I’m standing, where the door is, whether my back is turned. It feels kind of embarrassing to say because I know I’m supposed to just be doing my job, but I feel like I’m scanning the room all the time. So, yeah, my boss is definitely the person who seems to set that off the most.\n\nDoctor: Has something specific happened at work that you think might have triggered you to start feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah. It started after this late-night confrontation with my regional boss. That’s really the point where I noticed everything changed.\n\nIt’s been about two weeks now, maybe just over or right around that, but basically since that night. Before that, work was stressful in the normal way, you know, deadlines and people needing things, but I wasn’t walking into the office already bracing myself. After that confrontation, it was like my body started treating the office differently.\n\nI keep thinking, “Okay, it’s been two weeks, I should be over it,” but that’s the marker in my mind. That late-night thing with him happened, and since then I’ve felt this shift when I’m there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel less anxious at work if your boss were no longer there?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I probably would, at least somewhat. I don’t know if it would make the office feel completely normal again, because now I’m kind of keyed into the whole place — like the hallways, the exits, who’s behind me, that kind of thing. But he’s definitely the main thing I keep checking for.\n\nLike when I’m there, even if I’m supposed to be focused on my team or listening to someone talk, part of me is still going, “Where is he? Is he in his office? Is he coming out? Is he nearby?” And then I’m also looking at where the exits are or whether someone can come up behind me. It’s not like I’m trying to be dramatic about it, it’s just happening in the background all the time.\n\nSo if he weren’t there, I think that constant checking would probably drop down a lot. I might still feel tense in the office for a while, but I wouldn’t be tracking him every few seconds, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious if you were to encounter your boss in a setting outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think I probably would. I mean, I don’t know exactly because I haven’t really run into him outside of work like that, but I imagine my body would still react before I even had time to think it through.\n\nAt work, what happens is I keep checking where he is, even when I’m supposed to be focused on my team or having a normal conversation. Like part of me is still tracking him — where his office is, whether he’s coming down the hall, whether he’s behind me. And then it’s not just him, it’s also where the exits are, where I’m standing, if someone can come up behind me. It’s like I’m not fully in the room anymore because I’m doing all that in the background.\n\nSo if I saw him somewhere else, like a restaurant or a store or something, I think I’d probably start doing the same kind of thing — noticing where he is, where I am, how to get out, whether he’s coming closer. I don’t think I’d want to have that reaction, and I’d probably try to act normal, but yeah, I think I’d be anxious.\n\nDoctor: Did the late-night confrontation with your regional boss involve anything traumatic or threatening?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that, honestly. Like, I don’t know what qualifies as “traumatic” in the way you mean clinically.\n\nIt felt really intense to me, and I think the fact that it was late at night and it was my regional boss made it feel bigger, like I couldn’t just brush it off or walk away from it easily. But if you mean, was there some clear-cut thing where I can say, “Yes, that was a direct threat,” I don’t know that I can say that. I keep second-guessing myself about whether I’m making too much of it or whether it really was that bad.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is: I’m not sure. It felt threatening to me in the moment, or at least intimidating, but I don’t know if it fits what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Did the late-night confrontation with your regional boss involve any physical threats or harm?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there was actual physical harm, no. Like, he didn’t hit me or grab me or anything like that.\n\nAs far as physical threats… I’m not totally sure how to put it. I don’t remember him saying something clearly like, “I’m going to hurt you,” if that’s what you mean. It felt physically intimidating in the moment, but I don’t want to make it sound like there was a specific threat of violence if there wasn’t one.\n\nSo I guess the most accurate answer is: no actual physical harm, and I’m not sure there was a direct physical threat. It was more the way the situation felt — late at night, tense, hard to leave — but I can’t point to a clear physical threat.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Yeah… I think it probably would help some if I wasn’t directly under him anymore. Like, if I had a different supervisor and didn’t have to report to him or deal with him the same way, I imagine that would take the edge off.

But if I’m still in the same office, I don’t think it would just disappear. Because right now, when I’m there, I’m constantly sort of scanning without even meaning to. I’m checking where he is, whether he’s in his office, whether he’s walking around, whether he might come up behind me. And I keep noticing where the exits are or where I’m standing, even when I’m supposed to be paying attention to my team or just having a normal work conversation.

So a different role might make it better, yeah, especially if I didn’t have to interact with him as much. But if he’s still physically in the same building, I think part of me would still be on alert. It’s like my brain would still be tracking him in the background.

I keep second-guessing myself about whether I’m making too much of it or whether it really was that bad.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is: I’m not sure. It felt threatening to me in the moment, or at least intimidating, but I don’t know if it fits what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Did the late-night confrontation with your regional boss involve any physical threats or harm?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there was actual physical harm, no. Like, he didn’t hit me or grab me or anything like that.\n\nAs far as physical threats… I’m not totally sure how to put it. I don’t remember him saying something clearly like, “I’m going to hurt you,” if that’s what you mean. It felt physically intimidating in the moment, but I don’t want to make it sound like there was a specific threat of violence if there wasn’t one.\n\nSo I guess the most accurate answer is: no actual physical harm, and I’m not sure there was a direct physical threat. It was more the way the situation felt — late at night, tense, hard to leave — but I can’t point to a clear physical threat.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would decrease significantly if you were to switch roles or work under a different supervisor, but still work in the same office?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it probably would help some if I wasn’t directly under him anymore. I’m checking where he is, whether he’s in his office, whether he’s walking around, whether he might come up behind me. And I keep noticing where the exits are or where I’m standing, even when I’m supposed to be paying attention to my team or just having a normal work conversation.\n\nSo a different role might make it better, yeah, especially if I didn’t have to interact with him as much. But if he’s still physically in the same building, I think part of me would still be on alert. It’s like my brain would still be tracking him in the background.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your anxiety symptoms would meet the criteria for a panic attack if they were to occur in a setting outside of work?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 27 / first_line_supervisor_of_non_retail_sales_worker / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t really know where the line is between “really anxious” and what you would call a panic attack.

If it happened outside of work, I think I’d still probably describe it as anxiety or getting really keyed up, but I don’t know if it would meet criteria in the clinical sense. I haven’t really thought about it that way, like counting symptoms or timing it or anything. When it’s happening, I’m more focused on trying to get through it than figuring out what category it fits into.

So I guess my answer is: maybe, but I don’t know. I’d need you to explain what you mean by panic attack criteria before I could say that with any confidence.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.0, F41.1, F41.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I came in because things have been feeling kind of off, and what finally made me actually call EAP was this thing that keeps happening at work. Every little sudden noise in the office makes me jump way harder than it should. Like, if someone shuts a file drawer too loudly or drops a coffee mug in the break area, I don’t just flinch a little — I jump like something really serious happened.\n\nAnd then my chest starts racing, and it keeps going for way longer than makes sense. I know, logically, it’s just a drawer or a mug, but my body reacts like it’s a big deal before I can even think about it. It’s embarrassing too, because I’m a supervisor, so I’m supposed to be composed, you know? But lately these small bangs just set me off, and I don’t feel like I settle back down quickly. That’s why I thought I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Do these jumpiness and racing heart episodes only happen in response to sudden noises, or do they occur at other times as well?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the sudden noises, honestly. That’s the pattern I’ve noticed the clearest. Like, it isn’t that I’m just sitting there and my heart randomly starts pounding out of nowhere—at least not that I’ve really tracked. What made me call EAP was that every little bang in the office, like a file drawer shutting too hard or someone dropping a coffee mug, makes me jump way more than it should.\n\nAnd it’s not just a quick startle and then I’m fine. My chest starts racing, and it keeps going longer than makes sense for something so small. I’ll be trying to act normal, like I’m still focused on whatever work conversation is happening, but physically I’m still kind of keyed up. So… yeah, I’d say it’s mainly in response to sudden noises, at least that’s what I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or on edge in situations that are not related to sudden noises?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, it’s not as dramatic as the sudden noise thing, but I’ve noticed I’m kind of on alert in the office in general now.\n\nLike, when I’m supposed to be focused on my team or just getting through the day, part of my attention is somewhere else. I keep checking where my boss is, which is not something I used to do so much. I’ll catch myself noticing if he’s in his office, or walking around, or if I hear footsteps nearby. And I also keep clocking where the exits are, which sounds weird saying it out loud because I know I’m just at work, but it’s like I want to know how I’d get out if I needed to.\n\nAnd another thing is I really don’t like when people come up behind me now. If I’m at my desk or talking with someone, I’ll sort of position myself so I can see the hallway or see who’s coming. It’s not like I’m thinking through it in a calm, logical way every time — it’s more like I realize I’ve been scanning the room or checking behind me instead of actually listening as well as I should be. So yeah, I guess I do feel on edge even when there isn’t a noise. It’s just this constant checking.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel this way, on edge and constantly checking your surroundings, when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way, no. I mean, I can be a little wound up in general, but the constant checking thing is mainly when I’m in the office.\n\nThat’s where I notice it the most — I’m supposed to be focused on my team or whatever I’m working on, and instead I’m tracking where my boss is, whether he’s in his office or moving around, where the exits are, and if anyone is coming up behind me. I’ll catch myself positioning my chair or standing in a way where I can see the hallway, and it’s like I’m not even fully choosing to do it.\n\nOutside of work, I don’t think I’m scanning like that as much. It’s the office setting that seems to bring it out.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode where you felt an intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes, with symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, or feeling like you were going to pass out?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re describing, where it just suddenly comes on and peaks within a few minutes like a full episode.\n\nI mean, my heart does race when I get startled by a loud noise, like I was saying, and I feel really uncomfortable and keyed up after that. But if you mean something where I’m just sitting there and then suddenly I’m overwhelmed with fear, sweating, feeling like I might pass out or something like that… I can’t really say that’s happened.\n\nAt least, I haven’t noticed it as a separate kind of episode. It’s more tied to being startled or being on edge at work, not like random waves that peak out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or on edge at home or in social situations outside of work?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… or at least not in that same specific way. Like, at home I’m not constantly checking doors or watching where people are. And if I’m out with friends or just in a normal social situation, I don’t think I’m scanning the room like that.\n\nThe thing I keep coming back to is the office. That’s where it feels like I can’t fully settle. I’ll be trying to pay attention to my team or whatever’s going on, and then I realize I’m also tracking where my boss is — like whether he’s in his office or moving around — and I’m aware of where the exits are. I also really notice if someone is coming up behind me, and I’ll kind of position myself so that doesn’t happen, or so I can see it coming.\n\nSo outside of work, I might feel a little tense sometimes, but the constant checking, the “where is everyone, how do I get out, who’s behind me” thing… that’s mostly at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about work-related issues when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate that out. I mean, I’ll think about work when I’m not there, but I don’t know if I’d call it worrying in the sense of sitting at home spiraling about emails or sales numbers or normal work stuff.\n\nIt’s more like… if I know I have to go in the next day, I might find myself thinking ahead to what the office is going to feel like, or whether things are going to be awkward, or whether I’m going to be able to just get through the day without feeling so tense. But I wouldn’t say it’s constant all evening or anything like that.\n\nSo, yeah, work crosses my mind outside of work, but I haven’t really noticed it being this big separate thing where I’m obsessing over work problems at home. It’s more tied to having to go back there, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety at work more about not doing your job well or is it more about being in the office itself?\n\nPatient: It’s more about being in the office itself, I think. Not that I don’t care about doing my job well — I do, and I still try to focus on my team and what we’re supposed to be doing — but the anxiety doesn’t feel like, “Oh no, I’m going to mess up this report,” or “I’m not performing well enough.”\n\nIt’s more like once I’m physically there, in the office, I can’t fully relax into what I’m doing. I’ll be talking to someone or trying to listen in a meeting, and part of my attention is still somewhere else, like checking where my boss is, whether he’s in his office or walking around, where the exits are, and whether someone is coming up behind me. I know that probably sounds excessive, but it just feels automatic now.\n\nSo, yeah, I’d say it’s less about my actual job performance and more about being in that space and feeling like I have to keep track of everything around me.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific in the office that you think might be causing you to feel this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if it’s one object or one room exactly, but it’s definitely tied to being there, in the office, and especially to where my boss is. Like if I’m at my desk or talking to someone on my team, part of me is still checking whether he’s in his office, whether he’s walking around, whether he’s nearby. It’s like I’m trying to keep a mental map of him without meaning to.\n\nAnd then it’s the layout too, I guess — where the exits are, who’s behind me, whether someone could come up behind me without me noticing. I’ll choose where I stand or sit based on that sometimes, like so I can see the hallway or not have my back totally exposed. Even when I’m supposed to just be focused on my team or a conversation, some part of my attention is off tracking all of that.\n\nSo I don’t know if I’d say there’s one specific thing causing it, but being in that office now makes me feel like I have to keep checking everything.\n\nDoctor: Is there a particular person at work, such as your boss, who triggers your anxiety or makes you feel more on edge?\n\nPatient: Yeah… my boss, mostly. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m blaming him for every single thing I feel at work, because it’s also the office itself now, like the whole setup feels different to me. But if you’re asking whether there’s a specific person who makes me more on edge, then yes, it’s him.\n\nWhen I’m in the office now, I keep checking where he is without really deciding to do it. Like I’ll be trying to talk to someone on my team or focus on whatever we’re working on, and part of my brain is still tracking, “Is he in his office? Is he walking down the hall? Is he behind me?” It’s not like I’m sitting there staring at him, but I’m aware of him in this constant way.\n\nAnd then along with that, I’m also checking the exits and whether anyone is coming up behind me. I’ll notice where I’m standing, where the door is, whether my back is turned. It feels kind of embarrassing to say because I know I’m supposed to just be doing my job, but I feel like I’m scanning the room all the time. So, yeah, my boss is definitely the person who seems to set that off the most.\n\nDoctor: Has something specific happened at work that you think might have triggered you to start feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah. It started after this late-night confrontation with my regional boss. That’s really the point where I noticed everything changed.\n\nIt’s been about two weeks now, maybe just over or right around that, but basically since that night. Before that, work was stressful in the normal way, you know, deadlines and people needing things, but I wasn’t walking into the office already bracing myself. After that confrontation, it was like my body started treating the office differently.\n\nI keep thinking, “Okay, it’s been two weeks, I should be over it,” but that’s the marker in my mind. That late-night thing with him happened, and since then I’ve felt this shift when I’m there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel less anxious at work if your boss were no longer there?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I probably would, at least somewhat. I don’t know if it would make the office feel completely normal again, because now I’m kind of keyed into the whole place — like the hallways, the exits, who’s behind me, that kind of thing. But he’s definitely the main thing I keep checking for.\n\nLike when I’m there, even if I’m supposed to be focused on my team or listening to someone talk, part of me is still going, “Where is he? Is he in his office? Is he coming out? Is he nearby?” And then I’m also looking at where the exits are or whether someone can come up behind me. It’s not like I’m trying to be dramatic about it, it’s just happening in the background all the time.\n\nSo if he weren’t there, I think that constant checking would probably drop down a lot. I might still feel tense in the office for a while, but I wouldn’t be tracking him every few seconds, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious if you were to encounter your boss in a setting outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think I probably would. I mean, I don’t know exactly because I haven’t really run into him outside of work like that, but I imagine my body would still react before I even had time to think it through.\n\nAt work, what happens is I keep checking where he is, even when I’m supposed to be focused on my team or having a normal conversation. Like part of me is still tracking him — where his office is, whether he’s coming down the hall, whether he’s behind me. And then it’s not just him, it’s also where the exits are, where I’m standing, if someone can come up behind me. It’s like I’m not fully in the room anymore because I’m doing all that in the background.\n\nSo if I saw him somewhere else, like a restaurant or a store or something, I think I’d probably start doing the same kind of thing — noticing where he is, where I am, how to get out, whether he’s coming closer. I don’t think I’d want to have that reaction, and I’d probably try to act normal, but yeah, I think I’d be anxious.\n\nDoctor: Did the late-night confrontation with your regional boss involve anything traumatic or threatening?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that, honestly. Like, I don’t know what qualifies as “traumatic” in the way you mean clinically.\n\nIt felt really intense to me, and I think the fact that it was late at night and it was my regional boss made it feel bigger, like I couldn’t just brush it off or walk away from it easily. But if you mean, was there some clear-cut thing where I can say, “Yes, that was a direct threat,” I don’t know that I can say that. I keep second-guessing myself about whether I’m making too much of it or whether it really was that bad.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is: I’m not sure. It felt threatening to me in the moment, or at least intimidating, but I don’t know if it fits what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Did the late-night confrontation with your regional boss involve any physical threats or harm?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there was actual physical harm, no. Like, he didn’t hit me or grab me or anything like that.\n\nAs far as physical threats… I’m not totally sure how to put it. I don’t remember him saying something clearly like, “I’m going to hurt you,” if that’s what you mean. It felt physically intimidating in the moment, but I don’t want to make it sound like there was a specific threat of violence if there wasn’t one.\n\nSo I guess the most accurate answer is: no actual physical harm, and I’m not sure there was a direct physical threat. It was more the way the situation felt — late at night, tense, hard to leave — but I can’t point to a clear physical threat.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would decrease significantly if you were to switch roles or work under a different supervisor, but still work in the same office?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it probably would help some if I wasn’t directly under him anymore. Like, if I had a different supervisor and didn’t have to report to him or deal with him the same way, I imagine that would take the edge off.\n\nBut if I’m still in the same office, I don’t think it would just disappear. It’s like my brain would still be tracking him in the background.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety symptoms would meet the criteria for a panic attack if they were to occur in a setting outside of work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t really know where the line is between “really anxious” and what you would call a panic attack.\n\nIf it happened outside of work, I think I’d still probably describe it as anxiety or getting really keyed up, but I don’t know if it would meet criteria in the clinical sense. I haven’t really thought about it that way, like counting symptoms or timing it or anything. When it’s happening, I’m more focused on trying to get through it than figuring out what category it fits into.\n\nSo I guess my answer is: maybe, but I don’t know. Even when I’m supposed to just be focused on my team or a conversation, some part of my attention is off tracking all of that.\n\nSo I don’t know if I’d say there’s one specific thing causing it, but being in that office now makes me feel like I have to keep checking everything.\n\nDoctor: Is there a particular person at work, such as your boss, who triggers your anxiety or makes you feel more on edge?\n\nPatient: Yeah… my boss, mostly. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m blaming him for every single thing I feel at work, because it’s also the office itself now, like the whole setup feels different to me. But if you’re asking whether there’s a specific person who makes me more on edge, then yes, it’s him.\n\nWhen I’m in the office now, I keep checking where he is without really deciding to do it. Like I’ll be trying to talk to someone on my team or focus on whatever we’re working on, and part of my brain is still tracking, “Is he in his office? Is he walking down the hall? Is he behind me?” It’s not like I’m sitting there staring at him, but I’m aware of him in this constant way.\n\nAnd then along with that, I’m also checking the exits and whether anyone is coming up behind me. I’ll notice where I’m standing, where the door is, whether my back is turned. It feels kind of embarrassing to say because I know I’m supposed to just be doing my job, but I feel like I’m scanning the room all the time. So, yeah, my boss is definitely the person who seems to set that off the most.\n\nDoctor: Has something specific happened at work that you think might have triggered you to start feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah. It started after this late-night confrontation with my regional boss. That’s really the point where I noticed everything changed.\n\nIt’s been about two weeks now, maybe just over or right around that, but basically since that night. Before that, work was stressful in the normal way, you know, deadlines and people needing things, but I wasn’t walking into the office already bracing myself. After that confrontation, it was like my body started treating the office differently.\n\nI keep thinking, “Okay, it’s been two weeks, I should be over it,” but that’s the marker in my mind. That late-night thing with him happened, and since then I’ve felt this shift when I’m there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel less anxious at work if your boss were no longer there?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I probably would, at least somewhat. I don’t know if it would make the office feel completely normal again, because now I’m kind of keyed into the whole place — like the hallways, the exits, who’s behind me, that kind of thing. But he’s definitely the main thing I keep checking for.\n\nLike when I’m there, even if I’m supposed to be focused on my team or listening to someone talk, part of me is still going, “Where is he? Is he in his office? Is he coming out? Is he nearby?” And then I’m also looking at where the exits are or whether someone can come up behind me. It’s not like I’m trying to be dramatic about it, it’s just happening in the background all the time.\n\nSo if he weren’t there, I think that constant checking would probably drop down a lot. I might still feel tense in the office for a while, but I wouldn’t be tracking him every few seconds, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious if you were to encounter your boss in a setting outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think I probably would. I mean, I don’t know exactly because I haven’t really run into him outside of work like that, but I imagine my body would still react before I even had time to think it through.\n\nAt work, what happens is I keep checking where he is, even when I’m supposed to be focused on my team or having a normal conversation. Like part of me is still tracking him — where his office is, whether he’s coming down the hall, whether he’s behind me. And then it’s not just him, it’s also where the exits are, where I’m standing, if someone can come up behind me. It’s like I’m not fully in the room anymore because I’m doing all that in the background.\n\nSo if I saw him somewhere else, like a restaurant or a store or something, I think I’d probably start doing the same kind of thing — noticing where he is, where I am, how to get out, whether he’s coming closer. I don’t think I’d want to have that reaction, and I’d probably try to act normal, but yeah, I think I’d be anxious.\n\nDoctor: Did the late-night confrontation with your regional boss involve anything traumatic or threatening?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that, honestly. Like, I don’t know what qualifies as “traumatic” in the way you mean clinically.\n\nIt felt really intense to me, and I think the fact that it was late at night and it was my regional boss made it feel bigger, like I couldn’t just brush it off or walk away from it easily. But if you mean, was there some clear-cut thing where I can say, “Yes, that was a direct threat,” I don’t know that I can say that. I keep second-guessing myself about whether I’m making too much of it or whether it really was that bad.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is: I’m not sure. It felt threatening to me in the moment, or at least intimidating, but I don’t know if it fits what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Did the late-night confrontation with your regional boss involve any physical threats or harm?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there was actual physical harm, no. I don’t remember him saying something clearly like, “I’m going to hurt you,” if that’s what you mean. It felt physically intimidating in the moment, but I don’t want to make it sound like there was a specific threat of violence if there wasn’t one.\n\nSo I guess the most accurate answer is: no actual physical harm, and I’m not sure there was a direct physical threat. It was more the way the situation felt — late at night, tense, hard to leave — but I can’t point to a clear physical threat.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would decrease significantly if you were to switch roles or work under a different supervisor, but still work in the same office?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it probably would help some if I wasn’t directly under him anymore. I haven’t really thought about it that way, like counting symptoms or timing it or anything. When it’s happening, I’m more focused on trying to get through it than figuring out what category it fits into.\n\nSo I guess my answer is: maybe, but I don’t know. I think… yes, probably. Not in exactly the same way as when I’m at work, because the office itself is kind of where it happens the most, but I don’t think it’s only about work tasks or meetings or emails or anything like that.

If I’m at my desk or talking with someone, I’ll sort of position myself so I can see the hallway or see who’s coming. It’s not like I’m thinking through it in a calm, logical way every time — it’s more like I realize I’ve been scanning the room or checking behind me instead of actually listening as well as I should be. It’s just this constant checking.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel this way, on edge and constantly checking your surroundings, when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way, no. It’s the office setting that seems to bring it out.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode where you felt an intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes, with symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, or feeling like you were going to pass out?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re describing, where it just suddenly comes on and peaks within a few minutes like a full episode.\n\nI mean, my heart does race when I get startled by a loud noise, like I was saying, and I feel really uncomfortable and keyed up after that. But if you mean something where I’m just sitting there and then suddenly I’m overwhelmed with fear, sweating, feeling like I might pass out or something like that… I can’t really say that’s happened.\n\nAt least, I haven’t noticed it as a separate kind of episode. It’s more tied to being startled or being on edge at work, not like random waves that peak out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or on edge at home or in social situations outside of work?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… or at least not in that same specific way. Like, at home I’m not constantly checking doors or watching where people are. I also really notice if someone is coming up behind me, and I’ll kind of position myself so that doesn’t happen, or so I can see it coming.\n\nSo outside of work, I might feel a little tense sometimes, but the constant checking, the “where is everyone, how do I get out, who’s behind me” thing… that’s mostly at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about work-related issues when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate that out. I mean, I’ll think about work when I’m not there, but I don’t know if I’d call it worrying in the sense of sitting at home spiraling about emails or sales numbers or normal work stuff.\n\nIt’s more like… if I know I have to go in the next day, I might find myself thinking ahead to what the office is going to feel like, or whether things are going to be awkward, or whether I’m going to be able to just get through the day without feeling so tense. But I wouldn’t say it’s constant all evening or anything like that.\n\nSo, yeah, work crosses my mind outside of work, but I haven’t really noticed it being this big separate thing where I’m obsessing over work problems at home. It’s more tied to having to go back there, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety at work more about not doing your job well or is it more about being in the office itself?\n\nPatient: It’s more about being in the office itself, I think. Not that I don’t care about doing my job well — I do, and I still try to focus on my team and what we’re supposed to be doing — but the anxiety doesn’t feel like, “Oh no, I’m going to mess up this report,” or “I’m not performing well enough.”\n\nIt’s more like once I’m physically there, in the office, I can’t fully relax into what I’m doing. I’ll be talking to someone or trying to listen in a meeting, and part of my attention is still somewhere else, like checking where my boss is, whether he’s in his office or walking around, where the exits are, and whether someone is coming up behind me. I know that probably sounds excessive, but it just feels automatic now.\n\nSo, yeah, I’d say it’s less about my actual job performance and more about being in that space and feeling like I have to keep track of everything around me.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific in the office that you think might be causing you to feel this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if it’s one object or one room exactly, but it’s definitely tied to being there, in the office, and especially to where my boss is. Like if I’m at my desk or talking to someone on my team, part of me is still checking whether he’s in his office, whether he’s walking around, whether he’s nearby. It’s like I’m trying to keep a mental map of him without meaning to.\n\nAnd then it’s the layout too, I guess — where the exits are, who’s behind me, whether someone could come up behind me without me noticing. I’ll choose where I stand or sit based on that sometimes, like so I can see the hallway or not have my back totally exposed. Even when I’m supposed to just be focused on my team or a conversation, some part of my attention is off tracking all of that.\n\nSo I don’t know if I’d say there’s one specific thing causing it, but being in that office now makes me feel like I have to keep checking everything.\n\nDoctor: Is there a particular person at work, such as your boss, who triggers your anxiety or makes you feel more on edge?\n\nPatient: Yeah… my boss, mostly. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m blaming him for every single thing I feel at work, because it’s also the office itself now, like the whole setup feels different to me. But if you’re asking whether there’s a specific person who makes me more on edge, then yes, it’s him.\n\nWhen I’m in the office now, I keep checking where he is without really deciding to do it. Like I’ll be trying to talk to someone on my team or focus on whatever we’re working on, and part of my brain is still tracking, “Is he in his office? Is he walking down the hall? Is he behind me?” It’s not like I’m sitting there staring at him, but I’m aware of him in this constant way.\n\nAnd then along with that, I’m also checking the exits and whether anyone is coming up behind me. I’ll notice where I’m standing, where the door is, whether my back is turned. It feels kind of embarrassing to say because I know I’m supposed to just be doing my job, but I feel like I’m scanning the room all the time. So, yeah, my boss is definitely the person who seems to set that off the most.\n\nDoctor: Has something specific happened at work that you think might have triggered you to start feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah. It started after this late-night confrontation with my regional boss. That’s really the point where I noticed everything changed.\n\nIt’s been about two weeks now, maybe just over or right around that, but basically since that night. Before that, work was stressful in the normal way, you know, deadlines and people needing things, but I wasn’t walking into the office already bracing myself. After that confrontation, it was like my body started treating the office differently.\n\nI keep thinking, “Okay, it’s been two weeks, I should be over it,” but that’s the marker in my mind. That late-night thing with him happened, and since then I’ve felt this shift when I’m there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel less anxious at work if your boss were no longer there?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I probably would, at least somewhat. I don’t know if it would make the office feel completely normal again, because now I’m kind of keyed into the whole place — like the hallways, the exits, who’s behind me, that kind of thing. But he’s definitely the main thing I keep checking for.\n\nLike when I’m there, even if I’m supposed to be focused on my team or listening to someone talk, part of me is still going, “Where is he? Is he in his office? Is he coming out? Is he nearby?” And then I’m also looking at where the exits are or whether someone can come up behind me. It’s not like I’m trying to be dramatic about it, it’s just happening in the background all the time.\n\nSo if he weren’t there, I think that constant checking would probably drop down a lot. I might still feel tense in the office for a while, but I wouldn’t be tracking him every few seconds, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious if you were to encounter your boss in a setting outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think I probably would. I mean, I don’t know exactly because I haven’t really run into him outside of work like that, but I imagine my body would still react before I even had time to think it through.\n\nAt work, what happens is I keep checking where he is, even when I’m supposed to be focused on my team or having a normal conversation. Like part of me is still tracking him — where his office is, whether he’s coming down the hall, whether he’s behind me. And then it’s not just him, it’s also where the exits are, where I’m standing, if someone can come up behind me. It’s like I’m not fully in the room anymore because I’m doing all that in the background.\n\nSo if I saw him somewhere else, like a restaurant or a store or something, I think I’d probably start doing the same kind of thing — noticing where he is, where I am, how to get out, whether he’s coming closer. I don’t think I’d want to have that reaction, and I’d probably try to act normal, but yeah, I think I’d be anxious.\n\nDoctor: Did the late-night confrontation with your regional boss involve anything traumatic or threatening?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that, honestly. Like, I don’t know what qualifies as “traumatic” in the way you mean clinically.\n\nIt felt really intense to me, and I think the fact that it was late at night and it was my regional boss made it feel bigger, like I couldn’t just brush it off or walk away from it easily. But if you mean, was there some clear-cut thing where I can say, “Yes, that was a direct threat,” I don’t know that I can say that. I keep second-guessing myself about whether I’m making too much of it or whether it really was that bad.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is: I’m not sure. It felt threatening to me in the moment, or at least intimidating, but I don’t know if it fits what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Did the late-night confrontation with your regional boss involve any physical threats or harm?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there was actual physical harm, no. Like, he didn’t hit me or grab me or anything like that.\n\nAs far as physical threats… I’m not totally sure how to put it. I don’t remember him saying something clearly like, “I’m going to hurt you,” if that’s what you mean. It felt physically intimidating in the moment, but I don’t want to make it sound like there was a specific threat of violence if there wasn’t one.\n\nSo I guess the most accurate answer is: no actual physical harm, and I’m not sure there was a direct physical threat. It was more the way the situation felt — late at night, tense, hard to leave — but I can’t point to a clear physical threat.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would decrease significantly if you were to switch roles or work under a different supervisor, but still work in the same office?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it probably would help some if I wasn’t directly under him anymore. Like, if I had a different supervisor and didn’t have to report to him or deal with him the same way, I imagine that would take the edge off.\n\nBut if I’m still in the same office, I don’t think it would just disappear. Because right now, when I’m there, I’m constantly sort of scanning without even meaning to. I’m checking where he is, whether he’s in his office, whether he’s walking around, whether he might come up behind me. And I keep noticing where the exits are or where I’m standing, even when I’m supposed to be paying attention to my team or just having a normal work conversation.\n\nSo a different role might make it better, yeah, especially if I didn’t have to interact with him as much. But if he’s still physically in the same building, I think part of me would still be on alert. It’s like my brain would still be tracking him in the background.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety symptoms would meet the criteria for a panic attack if they were to occur in a setting outside of work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t really know where the line is between “really anxious” and what you would call a panic attack.\n\nIf it happened outside of work, I think I’d still probably describe it as anxiety or getting really keyed up, but I don’t know if it would meet criteria in the clinical sense. I haven’t really thought about it that way, like counting symptoms or timing it or anything. When it’s happening, I’m more focused on trying to get through it than figuring out what category it fits into.\n\nSo I guess my answer is: maybe, but I don’t know. I’d need you to explain what you mean by panic attack criteria before I could say that with any confidence.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious around your boss even if you were not at work and there was no work-related context?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably. I’d probably still be watching where he was standing, whether I had space to leave, that kind of thing. Yeah, I think I would. Maybe not as strongly as in the office, but I don’t think I’d feel neutral just because the setting was neutral.

Like, even now at work, it’s not only about him giving me an assignment or saying something about my performance. I keep finding myself tracking where he is, where the exits are, and whether someone might be coming up behind me, even when I’m supposed to be focused on my team or just doing normal work stuff.

So if I saw him in a store or a park, I think my first reaction would probably be to locate him, see how close he was, figure out if I could avoid him or leave if I needed to. I don’t think I’d just think, “Oh, there’s my boss,” and go on with my day. My body would probably go into that same kind of alert mode, even if nothing work-related was happening.

It felt physically intimidating in the moment, but I don’t want to make it sound like there was a specific threat of violence if there wasn’t one.\n\nSo I guess the most accurate answer is: no actual physical harm, and I’m not sure there was a direct physical threat. It was more the way the situation felt — late at night, tense, hard to leave — but I can’t point to a clear physical threat.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would decrease significantly if you were to switch roles or work under a different supervisor, but still work in the same office?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it probably would help some if I wasn’t directly under him anymore. Like, if I had a different supervisor and didn’t have to report to him or deal with him the same way, I imagine that would take the edge off.\n\nBut if I’m still in the same office, I don’t think it would just disappear. Because right now, when I’m there, I’m constantly sort of scanning without even meaning to. I’m checking where he is, whether he’s in his office, whether he’s walking around, whether he might come up behind me. And I keep noticing where the exits are or where I’m standing, even when I’m supposed to be paying attention to my team or just having a normal work conversation.\n\nSo a different role might make it better, yeah, especially if I didn’t have to interact with him as much. But if he’s still physically in the same building, I think part of me would still be on alert. It’s like my brain would still be tracking him in the background.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety symptoms would meet the criteria for a panic attack if they were to occur in a setting outside of work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t really know where the line is between “really anxious” and what you would call a panic attack.\n\nIf it happened outside of work, I think I’d still probably describe it as anxiety or getting really keyed up, but I don’t know if it would meet criteria in the clinical sense. I haven’t really thought about it that way, like counting symptoms or timing it or anything. When it’s happening, I’m more focused on trying to get through it than figuring out what category it fits into.\n\nSo I guess my answer is: maybe, but I don’t know. I’d need you to explain what you mean by panic attack criteria before I could say that with any confidence.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious around your boss even if you were not at work and there was no work-related context?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably. Not in exactly the same way as when I’m at work, because the office itself is kind of where it happens the most, but I don’t think it’s only about work tasks or meetings or emails or anything like that.\n\nLike, when I’m in the office now, I’m not just worried about a project or whether he’s going to criticize something. I keep checking where he is. I notice if his door is open, if he’s walking around, if his voice is nearby. I catch myself looking toward the exits, or thinking about whether someone is coming up behind me, even when I’m supposed to be focused on my team or having a normal conversation.\n\nSo if I saw him somewhere outside of work, even if there was no work context, I think my body would still react. I’d probably still be watching where he was standing, whether I had space to leave, that kind of thing. I don’t know if it would be as intense, but I don’t think I’d feel normal or relaxed around him just because we weren’t in the office.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel anxious if you saw your boss in a completely neutral setting, like a park or a store, with no work-related context?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think I would. Maybe not as strongly as in the office, but I don’t think I’d feel neutral just because the setting was neutral.\n\nLike, even now at work, it’s not only about him giving me an assignment or saying something about my performance. I keep finding myself tracking where he is, where the exits are, and whether someone might be coming up behind me, even when I’m supposed to be focused on my team or just doing normal work stuff.\n\nSo if I saw him in a store or a park, I think my first reaction would probably be to locate him, see how close he was, figure out if I could avoid him or leave if I needed to. I don’t think I’d just think, “Oh, there’s my boss,” and go on with my day. My body would probably go into that same kind of alert mode, even if nothing work-related was happening.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I think his presence would set me off in some way pretty much anywhere. I don’t mean I’d necessarily react the exact same way every single time, or that every setting would feel as intense as being in the office, but… I don’t think it’s limited to work stuff anymore.

So if I saw him somewhere else, even with no work reason for him to be there, I think I’d still go into that same alert mode. Like I’d be figuring out where he was standing, how close he was, and how I could leave if I needed to. I wouldn’t just feel relaxed because it wasn’t work-related.

My chest starts racing, and it keeps going longer than makes sense for something so small. Like if I’m at my desk or talking to someone on my team, part of me is still checking whether he’s in his office, whether he’s walking around, whether he’s nearby. I’d probably still be watching where he was standing, whether I had space to leave, that kind of thing. I don’t know if it would be as intense, but I don’t think I’d feel normal or relaxed around him just because we weren’t in the office.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel anxious if you saw your boss in a completely neutral setting, like a park or a store, with no work-related context?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think I would. Maybe not as strongly as in the office, but I don’t think I’d feel neutral just because the setting was neutral.\n\nLike, even now at work, it’s not only about him giving me an assignment or saying something about my performance. I keep finding myself tracking where he is, where the exits are, and whether someone might be coming up behind me, even when I’m supposed to be focused on my team or just doing normal work stuff.\n\nSo if I saw him in a store or a park, I think my first reaction would probably be to locate him, see how close he was, figure out if I could avoid him or leave if I needed to. I don’t mean I’d necessarily react the exact same way every single time, or that every setting would feel as intense as being in the office, but… I don’t think it’s limited to work stuff anymore.\n\nAt the office, when he’s around, I keep doing this thing where I check where he is. Like if his door opens, or I hear his voice, or I notice him walking behind people, my attention goes straight to him. And then I’m also checking where the exits are, and whether anyone is coming up behind me, even when I’m supposed to be focused on my team or whatever conversation I’m having.\n\nSo if I saw him somewhere else, even with no work reason for him to be there, I think I’d still go into that same alert mode. Like I’d be figuring out where he was standing, how close he was, and how I could leave if I needed to. I wouldn’t just feel relaxed because it wasn’t work-related."
